Novel Kernel Models and Exact Representor Theory for Neural Networks Beyond the Over-Parameterized Regime
Alistair Shilton, Applied Artificial Intelligence Institute ( AI ) {\rm(A}{\rm I}{\rm)} Deakin University, Geelong, Australia, Sunil Gupta, Applied Artificial Intelligence Institute ( AI ) {\rm(A}{\rm I}{\rm)} Deakin University, Geelong, Australia, Santu Rana, Applied Artificial Intelligence Institute ( AI ) {\rm(A}{\rm I}{\rm)} Deakin University, Geelong, Australia, Svetha Venkatesh, Applied Artificial Intelligence Institute ( AI ) {\rm(A}{\rm I}{\rm)} Deakin University, Geelong, Australia

Abstract
This paper presents two models of neural-networks and their training applicable to neural networks of arbitrary width, depth and topology, assuming only finite-energy neural activations; and a novel representor theory for neural networks in terms of a matrix-valued kernel. The first model is exact (un-approximated) and global, casting the neural network as an elements in a reproducing kernel Banach space (RKBS); we use this model to provide tight bounds on Rademacher complexity. The second model is exact and local, casting the change in neural network function resulting from a bounded change in weights and biases (ie. a training step) in reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) in terms of a local-intrinsic neural kernel (LiNK). This local model provides insight into model adaptation through tight bounds on Rademacher complexity of network adaptation. We also prove that the neural tangent kernel (NTK) is a first-order approximation of the LiNK kernel. Finally, and noting that the LiNK does not provide a representor theory for technical reasons, we present an exact novel representor theory for layer-wise neural network training with unregularized gradient descent in terms of a local-extrinsic neural kernel (LeNK). This representor theory gives insight into the role of higher-order statistics in neural network training and the effect of kernel evolution in neural-network kernel models. Throughout the paper (a) feedforward ReLU networks and (b) residual networks (ResNet) are used as illustrative examples.



1
Introduction

The application of reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS
(
Aronszajn, 1950
)
) and
reproducing kernel Banach space (RKBS
(
Lin et al., 2022
;
Der & Lee, 2007
;
Zhang et al., 2009
;
Zhang & Zhang, 2012
;
Song et al., 2013
;
Sriperumbudur et al., 2011
;
Xu & Ye, 2014
)
) theory to the study of neural networks has a long history
(
Neal, 1996
;
Weinan et al., 2019
;
Parhi & Nowak, 2021
;
Lee et al., 2018
;
Matthews et al., 2018
;
Rahimi & Benjamin, 2009
;
Bach, 2014
;
Bach, 2017
;
Daniely et al., 2016
;
Daniely, 2017
;
Cho & Saul, 2009
;
Bartolucci et al., 2021
;
Spek et al., 2022
)
. Neural tangent
kernels (NTKs) are an exemplar of this approach, modeling training using a
first-order (tangent) model. This approach has led to a wide body of work on
convergence and generalization
(
Du et al., 2019b
;
Allen-Zhu et al., 2019
;
Du et al., 2019a
;
Zou et al., 2020
;
Zou & Gu, 2019
;
Arora et al., 2019b
;
Arora et al., 2019a
;
Cao & Gu, 2019
)
,
mostly focused on the wide-network (over-parameterized) limit.

Nevertheless, as noted in eg.
Arora et al. (2019b)
;
Lee et al. (2019)
;
Bai & Lee (2019)
, there are limits to the
descriptive powers of NTK models. In particular, a gap has been observed
between NTK-based predictions and actual performance
(
Arora et al., 2019b
;
Lee et al., 2019
)
, and
the validity of NTK models naturally breaks down outside of the
over-parameterized limit. Recently this has led to attempts to generalize NTK
models outside of the over-parameterized limit. For example
(
Bell et al., 2023
)
use
an exact pathwise kernel,
(
Shilton et al., 2023
)
presented an exact model for dense
feedforward neural networks in RKBS,
(
Bartolucci et al., 2023
;
Sanders, 2020
;
Parhi & Nowak, 2021
;
Unser, 2021
;
Unser, 2019
)
have
explored links to RKBS, and
(
Bai & Lee, 2019
)
explored higher-order approximations.
However the assumptions made (smoothness, over-parameterisation etc) and
approximations used limit application and raise the question of whether it is
possible to instead formulate
exact, non-approximate
models for neural
networks that may be used for similar ends.

In this paper we address this question by formulating two
exact
or

non-approximate
models for arbitrary neural networks, and an representor
theory (also exact) for layerwise, feedforward networks:

1.

Exact global model: for an arbitrary neural network topology with arbitrary
weights and biases, subject to very mild assumptions, we construct an
RKBS model of the form
{\bf f}({\bf x};{\Theta})=\left<{{\bm{\Psi}}}({{\Theta}}),{{\bm{\phi}}}({\bf x})\right]_{{\bf g}}
. We use this model to place bounds on
Rademacher complexity.

2.

Exact local model: for the same network, this time assuming some initial
weights and biases, we construct an RKHS type model of the change in the
operation of the neural network given a bounded change in weights and
biases. We prove that the NTK is a first-order approximation of the
kernel describing this RKHS and apply it to bounding the Rademacher
complexity of network adaptation.

3.

Exact representor theory: finally, we derive an exact representor
theory for the change in neural network operation due to a weight-step
in unregularized gradient descent training. This RKHS model includes
several novel features, including (a) kernel warping, which is the
effect of model change for finite steps, (b) cross (off-diagonal) terms
describing the interaction between features in the non-over-parameterised
case, and (c) higher-order terms and interactions between training
vectors in the representor theory. We show that the kernel for the
exact local model may be understood as an approximation of the kernel
used in the representor theory, and the NTK a more coarse approximation
still.

We also discuss the role of network initialization for our model and its
predictions, particularly in the wide-network limit, and give some results
specific for our feedforward ReLU and ResNet examples.

1.1
Notation

We use

\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
,

\mathbb{N}_{n}=\{0,1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
,

\mathbb{Z}_{+}=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
.

{\rm Span}(\mathcal{X})
is the linear span of
\mathcal{X}
.
Column vectors are
{\bf a},{\bf b},\ldots
(elements
a_{i},\ldots
).

{\bf a}_{{\sim}i}
is
{\bf a}
with element
i
removed.
Matrices are
{\bf A},{\bf B},\ldots
(elements
A_{i,i^{\prime}}
, rows
{\bf A}_{i:}
, columns
{\bf A}_{:i^{\prime}}
).

{\rm diag}_{i}({\bf A}_{i})
is a block-diagonal matrix with blocks
{\bf A}_{i}
.
Elementwise absolute and sign are
|{\bf a}|
and
\sgn({\bf a})
.

[a]_{+}=\max\{a,0\}
.
Hadamard and Kronecker products are
{\bf A}\odot{\bf B}
,
{\bf A}\otimes{\bf B}
.
Column- (Khatri-Rao) and row-wise (face-splitting) products are
{\bf A}\otimes^{\updownarrow}{\bf B}
,
{\bf A}\otimes^{\leftrightarrow}{\bf B}
.
Hadamard and Kronecker powers are
{\bf A}^{\odot r}=[A_{i,j}^{r}]_{i,j}
,
{\bf A}^{\otimes k}={\bf A}\otimes{\bf A}\otimes\overset{k\;{\rm times}}{\ldots}{\otimes}{\bf A}
.
The
n^{\rm th}
derivative of
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is
f^{(n)}
.
Inner and bilinear products are
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>
,
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right]
.

\left<{\bf a},{\bf a}^{\prime}\right>_{\bf g}=\sum_{i}g_{i}a_{i}a^{\prime}_{i}
for
{\bf g}>{\bf 0}
,

\left<{\bf a},{\bf a}^{\prime}\right]_{\bf g}=\sum_{i}g_{i}a_{i}a^{\prime}_{i}

\forall{\bf g}
,

\left<{\bf A},{\bf a}^{\prime}\right]_{\bf g}=[\left<{\bf A}_{:j},{\bf a}^{\prime}\right]_{\bf g}]_{j}
,

\left<{\bf A},{\bf a}^{\prime}\right]_{\bf G}=[\left<{\bf A}_{:j},{\bf a}^{\prime}\right]_{{\bf G}_{:j}}]_{j}
.
By default
\left<{\bf a},{\bf a}^{\prime}\right>=\left<{\bf a},{\bf a}^{\prime}\right>_{\bf 1}
.

\|{\bf a}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i}|a_{i}|^{2}
and

\|{\bf A}\|_{2}^{2}=\sup_{{\bf x}:\|{\bf x}\|_{2}=1}\|{\bf A}{\bf x}\|_{2}^{2}

are the Euclidean and spectral norms.
The (probabilist’s) Hermite polynomials
(
Abramowitz et al., 1972
;
Morse & Feshbach, 1953
;
Olver et al., 2010
;
Courant & Hilbert, 1937
)
are denoted
{H\!e}_{k}
and form an orthogonal basis for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},e^{-x^{2}})\!=\!\{\tau:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\;|\int_{\infty}^{\infty}|\tau(\zeta)|^{2}e^{-\zeta^{2}}d\zeta<\infty\}
.
Hermite numbers are
{\rm He}_{k}={H\!e}_{k}(0)
.

Indexing:

{\widetilde{\jmath}},j\in\mathbb{N}_{D}
indexes node,

{\widetilde{\imath}}_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}}
,
i_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{H^{[j]}}

(
{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}
,
H^{[j]}
are the fan-in/out of node
j
).

2
Setting and Assumptions

Consider neural networks defined by directed acyclic graphs (computational
skeletons
(
Daniely et al., 2016
)
) with
D
nodes indexed by
j\in\mathbb{N}_{D}
, with
output node
j=D-1
; a virtual input node
j=-1
; and directed edges
({\widetilde{\jmath}}\to j)\in(\mathbb{N}_{D}\cup\{-1\})^{2}
. Node
j
has antecedents
{\widetilde{\jmath}}\in{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}\subset\mathbb{N}_{D}\cup\{-1\}
,
in-degree
{\tilde{p}}^{[j]}=|{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}|
, fan-out
H^{[j]}
,
fan-in
{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}=\sum_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}\in{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}}H^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}
(
H^{[-1]}=n
,
H^{[D-1]}=m
). Overall:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{l}\left.\begin{array}[]{l}{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Theta}\right)={\bf x}^{[D-1]}\\
\end{array}\right.\;\;\;\;\left|\begin{array}[]{rl}{\tilde{\bf x}}^{[j]}&\!\!\!\!=\big[{\tilde{\bf x}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\big]_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}\in{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}},{\tilde{\bf x}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}=\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}({\bf x}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]})\\
{\bf x}^{[j]}&\!\!\!\!={\bf W}^{[j]{\rm T}}{\tilde{\bf x}}^{[j]}+{\gamma}{\bf b}^{[j]}\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\;\left(=\sum_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}\in{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}}{\bf W}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]{\rm T}}{\tilde{\bf x}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}+{\gamma}{\bf b}^{[j]}\right)\\
\end{array}\right\}\;\begin{array}[]{l}\forall j\in\mathbb{N}_{D},{\widetilde{\jmath}}\in{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}\\
{\bf x}^{[-1]}={\bf x}\\
\end{array}\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(1)

where
{\bf W}^{[j]}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}\times H^{[j]}}
,
{\bf W}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\in\mathbb{R}^{H^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\times H^{[j]}}
and
{\bf b}^{[j]}\in\mathbb{R}^{H^{[j]}}
are weights and biases and

\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
the neural activation
functions. We write

{\Theta}=\{{\bf W}^{[j]},{\bf b}^{[j]}:j\in\mathbb{N}_{D}\}
. We assume

\exists{\mu}^{[j]},{\beta}^{[j]}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, with high
probability
\geq 1-\epsilon
, simultaneously
\forall j\in\mathbb{N}_{D}
:

1.

Bounded inputs:

\|{\bf x}\|_{2}\leq 1
.

2.

Bounded weights and biases:

\|{\bf W}^{[j]}\|_{2}\leq{\mu}^{[j]}
,
\|{\bf b}^{[j]}\|_{2}\leq{\beta}^{[j]}
.

3.

Bounded activations:

\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},e^{-\zeta^{2}})
.

We denote the set of inputs satisfying these assumptions
\mathbb{X}
and the
set of weights and biases satisfying same
\mathbb{W}
, so
{\bf f}:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Given a training set
\mathcal{D}=\{({\bf x}_{\{i\}},{\bf y}_{\{i\}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}:i\in\mathbb{N}_{N}\}
we assume the goal of training
is to minimize the risk (for loss
L
, regularizer
r
, trade-off
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
):

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{l}{\Theta}^{\star}=\mathop{\rm argmin}\limits_{{\Theta}\in\mathbb{W}}\sum_{i}L\left({\bf f}\left({\bf x}_{\{i\}};{\Theta}\right)-{\bf y}_{\{i\}}\right)+\lambda r\left({\Theta}\right)\\
\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(2)

The following examples are used throughout
(
Glorot et al., 2011
;
He et al., 2016
)
:

1.

Feedforward ReLU: fully connected, unbiased, feedforward, ReLU activations:

\!{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}\!=\!\{j-1\}

\forall j
;

{\gamma}=b_{i_{j}}^{[j]}=0

\forall j
;

\tau^{[j-1,j]}(\zeta)=[\zeta]_{+}

\forall j\neq 0,D-1
;

\tau^{[-1,0]}(\zeta)=\tau^{[D-2,D-1]}(\zeta)=\zeta
.

2.

Residual Network (ResNet): unbiased, alternating ReLU/skip,

D\in 2\mathbb{Z}_{+}
;

{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}=\{j-1\}

\forall j
even;

{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}=\{j-1,j-2\}
,

{\bf W}^{[j]}=[{\bf W}_{C}^{[j]};{\bf I}]

\forall j
odd;

{\gamma}=b_{i_{j}}^{[j]}=0

\forall j
;

\tau^{[j-1,j]}(\zeta)=[\zeta]_{+}

\forall j\neq 0,D-1
;

\tau^{[j-2,j]}(\zeta)=\zeta

\forall j
odd;

\tau^{[-1,0]}(\zeta)=\tau^{[D-2,D-1]}(\zeta)=\zeta
.

With regard to the weight-bound assumptions, if
W_{{\widetilde{\imath}}_{j},i_{j}}^{[j]}\in\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{[j]2})
then
\|{\bf W}_{:i_{j}}^{[j]}\|_{2}^{2}\sim\sigma^{[j]2}\chi_{{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}}^{2}
.
So, w.h.p.
1-\epsilon
,

\|{\bf W}_{:i_{j}}^{[j]}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\sigma^{[j]2}({\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}+2{{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]\frac{1}{2}}}\ln({DH^{[j]}}/{2\epsilon})+2\ln({DH^{[j]}}/{\epsilon}))

simultaneously
\forall j,i_{j}

(
Laurent & Massart, 2000
, Lemma 1, pg 1325)
.
Given sufficiently strong regularization,
a wide-network, or early stopping, network weights may be expected to
remain close to their initialization, so the initialization can be used as
a basis for norm-bounds. For example:

1.

LeCun (
\sigma^{[j]2}=\frac{1}{H^{[j]}}
):

{\mu}^{[j]2}=\frac{{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}}{H^{[j]}}+\frac{2\sqrt{{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}}}{H^{[j]}}\left(\ln\left(\frac{DH^{[j]}}{2\epsilon}\right)+\ln\left(\frac{DH^{[j]}}{\epsilon}\right)\right)
.

2.

He (
\sigma^{[j]2}=\frac{1}{{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}}
):

{\mu}^{[j]2}=1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}}}\left(\ln\left(\frac{DH^{[j]}}{2\epsilon}\right)+\ln\left(\frac{DH^{[j]}}{\epsilon}\right)\right)
.

3.

Glorot (
\sigma^{[j]2}=\frac{1}{H^{[j]}+{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}}
):

{\mu}^{[j]2}=\frac{{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}}{H^{[j]}+{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}}+\frac{2\sqrt{{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}}}{{H^{[j]}}+{{\widetilde{H}}^{[j]}}}\left(\ln\left(\frac{DH^{[j]}}{2\epsilon}\right)+\ln\left(\frac{DH^{[j]}}{\epsilon}\right)\right)
.

The norm-bound for LeCun initialization varies with fan-in/out
and may be arbitrarily large. The norm-bound for He initialization is
better behaved with respect to fan-in but may be arbitrarily large depending on fan-out.
In contrast, the norm bound for Glorot initialization may be finitely bounded
by
{\mu}^{[j]2}\leq 1+{({\frac{5}{3}}({{\epsilon}/{D}})^{{1}/{2}}+{({{D}/{\epsilon}})^{{1}/{2}}}\ln 2)}\sqrt{2}e^{-{2\epsilon}/{3D}}
,
independent of both fan-in and fan-out, which is most helpful in our context
as it is well-behaved in the wide-network limit.

2.1
Related Work - NNGP, NTK and Beyond

The use of kernel methods to model neural networks dates to at least

(
Neal, 1996
)
. Fixing all weights and biases except for node
j
and defining
feature map
{{\bm{\varphi}}}^{[j]}({\bf x})=[{\tilde{\bf x}}^{[j]};{\gamma}]
,
the network can be written as
{\bf f}({\bf x};{\Theta})={\bf q}^{[j]}([{\bf W}^{[j]{\rm T}};{\bf b}^{[j]}]{{\bm{\varphi}}}^{[j]}({\bf x}),{\bf x})

for fixed
{\bf q}^{[j]}
, and (
2
) becomes kernel
regression with:

\begin{array}[]{l}\!\!\mbox{NNGP Kernel:}\begin{array}[]{l}\Sigma^{[j]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)=\mathbb{E}_{k}\left[\varphi_{k}^{[j]}\left({\bf x}\right)\varphi_{k}^{[j]}\left({\bf x}^{\prime}\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{{\tilde{j}}\in{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}}\left[\Sigma^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)\right]\\
\Sigma^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)=\gamma^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}\left[{\tilde{x}}_{i_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}{\tilde{x}}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\right]\\
\end{array}\end{array}

(3)

In the infinitely wide limit, for suitable initialization, (
3
)
is deterministic (dependent on the distribution
{\Theta}\sim\nu
),
and it can be demonstrated that
{\bf x}^{[j]}(\cdot)\sim{\rm GP}(0,\Sigma^{[j]})

(
Neal, 1996
;
Lee et al., 2018
;
Matthews et al., 2018
;
Garriga-Alonso et al., 2019
;
Novak et al., 2019
)
. This is the NNGP model, and
may be used to eg. derive insights into the types of function the network is
best suited to model. The NNGP kernel for our ReLU example is the arc-cosine
kernel
(
Cho & Saul, 2009
)
.

To model network training neural tangent kernels (NTKs
(
Jacot et al., 2018
;
Arora et al., 2019b
)
) are
a first-order model approximating the behavior of a neural network as the
weights and biases vary about their initialization
{\Theta}
, i.e.
{\bf f}({\bf x};{\Theta}+{\Delta}{\Theta})\approx{\bf f}({\bf x};{\Theta})+{\Delta}{\Theta}^{\rm T}\nabla_{\Theta}{\bf f}({\bf x};{\Theta})
. Training is cast as kernel regression, with the neural
tangent kernel (NTK):

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{l}\!\!\mbox{NTK:}\begin{array}[]{l}\left.\begin{array}[]{l}K_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm NTK}}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)=\mathbb{E}_{k}\left[\nabla_{{\Theta}_{k}}{\bf f}({\bf x};{\Theta})^{\rm T}\nabla_{{\Theta}_{k}}{\bf f}({\bf x};{\Theta})\right]=K_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm NTK}}^{[D-1]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)\end{array}\right.\\
\left.\begin{array}[]{rl}K_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm NTK}}^{[j]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)&\!\!\!\!=\Sigma^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)+\mathbb{E}_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}\in{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}}\left[\theta^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)K_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm NTK}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)\right]\\
\theta^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathbb{E}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}\left[\left<\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j](1)}\left({x}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\right)\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j](1)}\left({x}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}^{\prime[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\right)\right>\right]\;\forall{\widetilde{\jmath}}\in{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}\\
\end{array}\right\}\;\forall j\\
\end{array}\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(4)

and
K_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm NTK}}^{[-1]}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})=0
. The NTK model is accurate
for small variations in weights/biases. In the infinitely wide limit the NTK
is deterministic, and weights/biases remain close to their initial values,
leading to the gradient flow model where weights flow rather than change in
discrete steps. This approach gives insight in areas including convergence
and generalization
(
Du et al., 2019b
;
Allen-Zhu et al., 2019
;
Du et al., 2019a
;
Zou et al., 2020
;
Zou & Gu, 2019
;
Arora et al., 2019b
;
Arora et al., 2019a
;
Cao & Gu, 2019
)
.

Beyond this first-order model, while NTKs have made significant progress, a
gap has been observed between NTK-based predictions and actual performance

(
Arora et al., 2019b
;
Lee et al., 2019
)
. One approach to bridging this gap is to construct
higher-order or exact models. Works in this direction include
(
Bai & Lee, 2019
)
,
which presented a higher-order approximation;
(
Bell et al., 2023
)
, which used a
pathwise kernel; and
(
Shilton et al., 2023
)
, which used an RKBS model.
1
1

1

In a
similar vein,
(
Bartolucci et al., 2023
;
Sanders, 2020
;
Parhi & Nowak, 2021
;
Unser, 2021
;
Unser, 2019
)
explore the link to RKBS
theory, though excepting
(
Unser, 2019
)
they only consider shallow networks.

3
Hermite Representation of Neural Activations

The local and global models given here are based on a Hermite transform of the
activations. This transform of
\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}
is constructed around a

center

\xi
. We begin by defining the centered activations:

\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Centered activation:}\begin{array}[]{c}{\overline{\tau}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi\right)=\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\xi+\zeta\right)-\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\xi\right)\end{array}\end{array}

By assumption
\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},e^{-\zeta^{2}})
, so

{\overline{\tau}}^{[j^{\prime},j]}(\cdot,\xi)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},e^{-\zeta^{2}})
and the
Hermite transform
(
Abramowitz et al., 1972
;
Morse & Feshbach, 1953
;
Olver et al., 2010
)
exists. Precisely:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{rl}{\overline{\tau}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi\right)&\!\!\!\!=\sum_{k\geq 0}a^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}_{\left(\xi\right)k}{H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)=\sum_{k\geq 1}a_{\left(\xi\right)k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}\zeta^{l}\\
\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\mbox{where: }a_{\left(\xi\right)k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}k!}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\overline{\tau}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi\right){H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)e^{-{\zeta^{2}}/{2}}d\zeta\end{array}\\
\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(5)

where
{\rm He}_{k}={H\!e}_{k}(0)
are the Hermite numbers (in the
second form of
{\overline{\tau}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}
we use the additivity properties
of the Hermite polynomials (supplementary
A
)).
We define the
magnitude functions
:

\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Magnitude function:}\begin{array}[]{rl}{{{\overline{s}}}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta\right)&\!\!\!\!=\sum_{k\geq 0}\left|a_{(0)k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\right|\left(1+\zeta\right)^{k}-\sum_{k\geq 0}\left|a_{(0)k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\right|\\
\end{array}\end{array}

(6)

for the global model.
For the local model we have the
rectified activation functions
and
their envelopes:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{rl}\mbox{Rectified activation function:}&{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi,\xi^{\prime}\right)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{a_{(\xi)k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}a_{(\xi^{\prime})k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}}{\eta^{2k}}\sum_{l=1}^{k}{\binom{k}{l}}^{2}{\rm He}_{k-l}^{2}\zeta^{l}\\
\mbox{Envelope:}&{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;{\omega}\right)=\mathop{\rm sup}\limits_{|\xi|,|\xi^{\prime}|\leq{\omega}}{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi,\xi^{\prime}\right)=\mathop{\rm sup}\limits_{|\xi|\leq{\omega}}{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi,\xi\right)\\
\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(7)

where
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in[-{\omega},{\omega}]
are the centers of the rectified
activation functions and
\eta\in(0,1)
is fixed.
The magnitude
functions converge everywhere, while the rectified activations have a
finite ROC
|{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}(\zeta;\xi,\xi^{\prime})|\leq{{{T}}}_{(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}

\forall|\zeta|\leq{{{{\rho}}}}_{(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}
, and likewise
|{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}(\zeta;{\omega})|\leq{{{T}}}_{({\omega})\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}

\forall|\zeta|\leq{{{{\rho}}}}_{({\omega})\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}
- see supplementary
A.2
.
The magnitude function and rectified activation envelope
are origin crossing, monotonically
increasing and superadditive
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We also define (supplementary
A.2
):

\begin{array}[]{rl}{{{\hat{s}}}}_{\eta}\left(\zeta\right)&\!\!\!\!={\sum}_{k\geq 1}\eta^{2k}{\sum}_{1\leq l\leq k}\zeta^{l}=\frac{\zeta}{1-\zeta}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}}{1-\eta^{2}}-\frac{\zeta\eta^{2}}{1-\zeta\eta^{2}}\right)\end{array}

which converges as given
\forall|\zeta|<{{{{R}}}}_{\eta}^{2}<1
, whereon

{{{{S}}}}_{\eta}^{2}=\frac{{{{{R}}}}_{\eta}^{2}}{1-{{{{R}}}}_{\eta}^{2}}(\frac{\eta^{2}}{1-\eta^{2}}-\frac{\eta^{2}{{{{R}}}}_{\eta}^{2}}{1-\eta^{2}{{{{R}}}}_{\eta}^{2}})
.

For linear activations
\tau(\zeta)=\zeta
then
a_{(\xi)k}=\delta_{k,0}

and
{{{\overline{s}}}}(\zeta)=\zeta
,
{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}(\zeta;\xi,\xi^{\prime})=\zeta/\eta
. For
ReLU activations
\tau(\zeta)=[\zeta]_{+}
, as shown in supplementary

A.3
(where the case
\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}
is also given):

\begin{array}[]{rlrl}a_{\left(0\right)k}&\!\!\!\!=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\left(-1\right)^{p+1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}(2p-1)2^{p}p!}&\mbox{if }k=2p,p\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\\
\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k,1}&\mbox{otherwise}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{array}

(8)

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{rl}{{{\overline{s}}}}\left(\zeta\right)&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{2}\zeta\left({\rm erfi}\left(\frac{1+\zeta}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+1\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\Big(e^{\frac{1}{2}}-e^{\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\zeta\right)^{2}}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\left({\rm erfi}\left(\frac{1+\zeta}{\sqrt{2}}\right)-{\rm erfi}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right)\\
\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(9)

It is difficult to obtain a closed-form expression for the rectified activation
or its envelope for the ReLU, but they are relatively straightforward to
calculate, as are their convergence bounds. Figure
3
in
supplementary
A.3
shows a sample of various rectified activations
for the ReLU with different centers.

4
Global Dual Model in Reproducing Kernel Banach Space

In this section we derive a dual model for the network described, where by
global we mean not constructed about some weight initialization. Our
derivation is similar to
(
Shilton et al., 2023
)
, but based on a Hermite polynomial expansion
rather than a Taylor series, making it applicable to a wider range of activation
functions with fewer caveats. Our key result for this section is:

Theorem 1
.

Let
{\bf f}:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a neural
network (
1
) satisfying our assumptions. Then:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Theta}\right)&\!\!\!\!=\left<{{\bm{\Psi}}}\left({{\Theta}}\right),{{\bm{\phi}}}\left({\bf x}\right)\right]_{{\bf g}}\\
\end{array}

(10)

with feature maps

{{\bm{\Psi}}}:\mathbb{W}\to{\mathcal{W}}=\overline{\rm span}({{\bm{\Psi}}}(\mathbb{W}))

and

{{\bm{\phi}}}:\mathbb{X}\to{\mathcal{X}}=\overline{\rm span}({{\bm{\phi}}}(\mathbb{X}))

and metric
{\bf g}
defined in Figure
2
, where

\|{{\bm{\Psi}}}({\Theta})\|_{2}\leq{\psi}

and

{\phi}_{{{\downarrow}}}\leq\|{{\bm{\phi}}}({\bf x})\|_{2}\leq{\phi}

\forall{\Theta}\in\mathbb{W},{\bf x}\in\mathbb{X}
. Moreover:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{l}\left\|{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Theta}\right)\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|{{\bm{\Psi}}}\left({\Theta}\right)\right\|_{{\rm He}[\tau]}\left\|{{\bm{\phi}}}\left({\bf x}\right)\right\|_{2},\;\;\par\left\|{{\bm{\Psi}}}\right\|_{{\rm He}[\tau]}^{2}=\mathop{\rm sup}\limits_{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{X}}\frac{\left\|\left<{{\bm{\Psi}}},{{\bm{\phi}}}\left({\bf x}\right)\right]_{{\bf g}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{\left\|{{\bm{\phi}}}\left({\bf x}\right)\right\|_{2}}\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(11)

where
\|{{\bm{\Psi}}}({\Theta})\|_{{\rm He}[\tau]}\leq\undertilde{{\psi}}

\forall{\Theta}\in\mathbb{W}
, as per definitions in Figure
2
.

See supplementary
B
for a proof of this theorem.
Intuitively, this result may be derived recursively, starting from the input
node and progressing to the output, using the Hermite expansion of the
activation for the edges. The operator-norm based bound (
11
)
is included here due to the fact that the indefinite metric prevents us from
naively bound
\|{\bf f}({\bf x};{\Theta})\|_{2}
in terms of
{\phi}{\psi}
using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (as may be required e.g.
when bounding Rademacher complexity).

4.1
Implication: Neural Networks in Reproducing Kernel Banach Space

A reproducing kernel Banach space is defined as:

Definition 1

(Reproducing kernel Banach space (RKBS))
.

A reproducing kernel Banach space on a set
\mathbb{X}
is a Banach space

\mathcal{F}
of functions
{\bf f}:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{Y}
for which the
point evaluation functionals
{{\bm{\delta}}}_{\bf x}({\bf f})={\bf f}({\bf x})
on

\mathcal{F}
are continuous (i.e.
\forall{\bf x}\in\mathbb{X}

\exists C_{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\|{{\bm{\delta}}}_{\bf x}({\bf f})\|_{2}\leq C_{\bf x}\|{\bf f}\|_{\mathcal{F}}

\forall{\bf f}\in\mathcal{F}
).

This definition is somewhat generic, so
(
Lin et al., 2022
)
2
2

2

See e.g.

(
Der & Lee, 2007
;
Lin et al., 2022
;
Zhang et al., 2009
;
Zhang & Zhang, 2012
;
Song et al., 2013
;
Sriperumbudur et al., 2011
;
Xu & Ye, 2014
)
for other perspectives.
study
the special case:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{B}=\left\{\left.{\bf f}\left(\cdot;{\Theta}\right)=\left<{{\bm{\Psi}}}\left({\Theta}\right),{{\bm{\Phi}}}\left(\cdot\right)\right]_{\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{X}}\right|{\Theta}\in\mathbb{W}\right\}\\
\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(12)

where
{{\bm{\Phi}}}:\mathbb{X}\to\mathcal{X}
is a data feature map,

{{\bm{\Psi}}}:\mathbb{W}\to\mathcal{W}
is a weight feature map,

\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{W}
are
Banach spaces, and
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is continuous.
The following result follows from theorem

1
:
3
3

3

Note that the RKBS defined in theorem
2
is
non-reflexive, which appears to rule out a trivial representor theory based
on this dual in the global case
(
Lin et al., 2022
)
.

Theorem 2
.

The set

\mathcal{F}=\{{\bf f}(\cdot;{\Theta}):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}|{\Theta}\in\mathbb{W}\}

of networks (
1
) satisfying our assumptions forms
a RKBS of form (
12
), where

\|{\bf f}(\cdot;{\Theta})\|_{\mathcal{F}}=\|{{\bm{\Psi}}}({\Theta})\|_{{\rm He}[\tau]}\leq{{\undertilde{{\psi}}}}

and

C_{\bf x}=\|{{\bm{\phi}}}({\bf x})\|_{2}\leq{\phi}
.

4.2
Application: Bounding Rademacher Complexity for Neural Network Training

The global dual formulation may be used to bound Rademacher complexity, which
in turn bounds the uniform convergence properties of the network class

(
Bartlett & Mendelson, 2002
;
Steinwart & Christman, 2008
)
.
Assuming
{\bf x}\sim\nu
the Rademacher complexity is defined as

\mathcal{R}_{N}(\mathcal{F})=\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}[{\rm sup}_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{N}}\epsilon_{i}f({\bf x}_{i})]

for Rademacher random variables
\epsilon_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
. We have:

Theorem 3
.

The set
\mathcal{F}=\{f(\cdot;{\Theta}):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}|{\Theta}\in\mathbb{W}\}
of networks (
1
)
satisfying our assumptions has Rademacher complexity bounded by

\mathcal{R}_{N}(\mathcal{F})\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}{{\phi}}\undertilde{{\psi}}

(definitions as per Figure
2
).

The proof follows the usual template (see e.g.
(
Bartlett & Mendelson, 2002
)
) using our feature
map, with (
11
) used instead of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
(supplementary
D
). If

{\mu}^{[j]}
,
{\beta}^{[j]}
are width independent
(eg. after Glorot initialization) then so too is the bound. For Lipschitz
activations we have:

Theorem 4
.

Let
\mathcal{F}=\{f(\cdot;{\Theta}):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}|{\Theta}\in\mathbb{W}\}
be the set of unbiased networks
(
1
) with
L
-Lipschitz activations satisfying our
assumptions. Then

\mathcal{R}_{N}(\mathcal{F})\leq\max_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathbb{S}}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{S}}L^{2}{\tilde{p}}^{[j]}{\mu}^{[j]}
, where
\mathbb{S}

is the set of all input-output paths in the network graph.

We observe that this bound applies to both our ReLU and ResNet examples, as
both linear and ReLU activations are
1
-Lipschitz. Indeed, for a ReLU
network, assuming Glorot initialization and sufficiently strong (spectral or
equivalent) regularization we have
{\mu}^{[j]}\approx 1
, so

\mathcal{R}_{N}(\mathcal{F})\lessapprox{1}/{\sqrt{N}}
.

\hline\cr{\begin{array}[]{rl}{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Theta}+{\Delta}{\Theta}\right)&\!\!\!\!={\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Theta}\right)+{\Delta}{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Delta}{\Theta}\right)^{{\;}{}^{{\;}}}\\
{\Delta}{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Delta}{\Theta}\right)&\!\!\!\!=\left<{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{{\Delta}}\left({\Delta}{{\Theta}}\right),{{\bm{\phi}}}_{\Delta}\left({\bf x}\right)\right]_{{\bf G}_{\Delta}({\bf x})}^{{\;}{}^{{\;}{}^{\;}}}\\
\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{l}{{\bm{\phi}}}_{\Delta}={{\bm{\phi}}}_{{\Delta}}^{[D-1]},{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{\Delta}={{\bm{\Psi}}}_{{\Delta}}^{[D-1]},{\bf G}_{\Delta}={\bf G}_{{\Delta}}^{{[D-1]}^{{\;}{}^{{\;}{}^{\;}}}}\\
{\phi}_{{\Delta}}={\phi}_{{\Delta}}^{[D-1]},{\psi}_{{\Delta}}={\psi}_{{\Delta}}^{[D-1]}\\
\end{array}}\end{array}

Figure 2:
Recursive definition of local dual and bounds.
See theorem
5
, section
5
for details.

5
Local Dual Model in Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space

When considering training or network adaptation it is better to model the
change in the network rather than the network in-toto. To this end, in this
section we present an exact (non-approximate) local RKHS model.
Let
{\Theta}
be the initial weight and biases (before a training step)
and
{\Delta}{\Theta}
the change in weights and biases (the training
step). Let
{\tilde{\bf x}}^{[j]},{\bf x}^{[j]}
denote the
pre-activation (input) and post-activation (output) of node
j
with
initial weights given input
{\bf x}
; and
{\Delta}{\tilde{\bf x}}^{[j]},{\Delta}{\bf x}^{[j]}
the change in same due to the
change in weights. The change in network operation is denoted
{\Delta}{\bf f}:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{W}_{\Delta}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Theta}+{\Delta}{\Theta}\right)={\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Theta}\right)+{\Delta}{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Delta}{\Theta}\right),\end{array}

(13)

Simultaneously with previous assumptions, whp
\geq 1-\epsilon
, we assume

\exists{\mu}_{\Delta}^{[j]},{\beta}_{\Delta}^{[j]},\tilde{{\omega}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
so that:

4.

Finite base activation:
\|{\tilde{\bf x}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\|_{2}\leq\tilde{{\omega}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}

\forall{\widetilde{\jmath}}\in{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}

(note that
\tilde{{\omega}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\leq{{\tilde{\phi}}}{}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\undertilde{{\tilde{\psi}}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}
).

5.

Finite weight and bias step:
\|{\Delta}{\bf W}^{[j]}\|_{2}\leq{\mu}_{\Delta}^{[j]}
,
\|{\Delta}{\bf b}^{[j]}\|_{2}\leq{\beta}_{\Delta}^{[j]}
.

We define
\mathbb{W}_{\Delta}
to be the set of all weight-steps satisfying
these assumptions. The parameter
\tilde{{\omega}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}
is a bound
on the magnitude of the output of edge
({\widetilde{\jmath}}\to j)
in our initial network

\forall{\bf x}\in\mathbb{X}
. With this prequel, we have the following
local analogue of theorem
1
(see proof in supplementary

C
):
4
4

4

The decision to use a position dependent
metric
{\bf G}_{\Delta}
here is largely stylistic. We could of course
absorb
{\bf G}_{\Delta}
into
{{\bm{\phi}}}_{\Delta}
without
substantively changing our results.

Theorem 5
.

Let
{\Delta}{\bf f}:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{W}_{\Delta}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

be the change in neural network operation (
13
). Then:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\Delta}{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Delta}{\Theta}\right)&\!\!\!\!=\left<{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{{\Delta}}\left({\Delta}{{\Theta}}\right),{{\bm{\phi}}}_{\Delta}\left({\bf x}\right)\right]_{{\bf G}_{\Delta}({\bf x})}\\
\end{array}

(14)

with feature maps

{{\bm{\phi}}}_{\Delta}:\mathbb{X}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{\Delta}=\overline{\rm span}({{\bm{\phi}}}_{\Delta}(\mathbb{X}))
,

{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{\Delta}:\mathbb{W}_{\Delta}\to{\mathcal{W}}_{\Delta}=\overline{\rm span}({{\bm{\Psi}}}_{\Delta}(\mathbb{W}_{\Delta}))

and metric

{\bf G}_{\Delta}({\bf x})

as per Figure
2
, where

\|{{\bm{\phi}}}_{\Delta}({\bf x})\odot{\bf G}_{{\Delta}:i_{D-1}}({\bf x})\|_{2}\leq{\phi}_{{\Delta}}

\forall i_{D-1}

and

\|{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{\Delta}({\Delta}{\Theta})\|_{2}\leq{\psi}_{{\Delta}}

\forall{\bf x}\in\mathbb{X}
,
{\Delta}{\Theta}\in\mathbb{W}_{\Delta}
.
Moreover

\|{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{\Delta}({\Delta}{\Theta})\|_{2}\leq{\psi}_{{\Delta}}={{{{S}}}}_{\eta}^{2}<1

if
\forall j
:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{rl}{\mu}_{\Delta}^{[j]2}+\frac{1}{3{\tilde{p}}^{[j]}\tilde{{\omega}}^{[j]2}}{\beta}_{\Delta}^{[j]2}\leq\frac{u^{[j]2}}{6{\tilde{p}}^{[j]}\tilde{{\omega}}^{[j]2}}\;:\;\;\;u^{[j]2}=\mathop{\min}\limits_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}:j\in{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}}\frac{1}{{\gamma}^{2}+1}{{{{\rho}}}}_{(\tilde{{\omega}}^{[j,{\widetilde{\jmath}}]})\eta}^{[j,{\widetilde{\jmath}}]2}\left\{{{{{R}}}}_{\eta}^{2},{{{\hat{s}}}}_{\eta}^{-1}\left(\frac{u^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]2}}{12{\tilde{p}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}{\mu}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]2}}\right)\right\}\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(15)

5.1
Implication: Neural Network Change in Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space

A vector-valued (v-v) reproducing kernel Hilbert space is defined as follows

(
Aronszajn, 1950
;
Steinwart & Christman, 2008
;
Shawe-Taylor & Cristianini, 2004
;
Mercer, 1909
;
Micchelli & Pontil, 2005
;
Caponnetto & De Vito, 2007
;
Reisert & Burkhardt, 2007
;
Carmeli et al., 2005
;
Schwartz, 1964
)
:

Definition 2

(Reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS))
.

A v-v reproducing kernel Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
on a set
\mathbb{X}
is a
Hilbert space
\mathcal{F}
of functions
{\bf f}:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
for which the point evaluation functionals
{{\bm{\delta}}}_{\bf x}({\bf f})={\bf f}({\bf x})
on
\mathcal{F}
are continuous (
\forall{\bf x}\in\mathbb{X}

\exists C_{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
s.t.

\|{{\bm{\delta}}}_{\bf x}({\bf f})\|_{2}\leq C_{\bf x}\|{\bf f}\|_{\mathcal{F}}

\forall{\bf f}\in\mathcal{F}
).

For an RKHS, Reisz representor theory implies that
\forall{\bf x}\in\mathbb{X}

\exists
unique
{\bf K}_{\bf x}\in\mathcal{F}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
\left<{\bf f}({\bf x}),{\bf v}\right>=\left<{\bf f},{\bf K}_{\bf x}{\bf v}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}

\forall{\bf v}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. From this, the kernel
{\bf K}:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
is defined as:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\bf K}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)=\Big[\left<{\bf K}_{\bf x}{{\bm{\delta}}}{}_{(i_{D-1})}^{[D-1]},{\bf K}_{{\bf x}^{\prime}}{{\bm{\delta}}}{}_{(i^{\prime}_{D-1})}^{[D-1]}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}\Big]{}_{{{}\atop{\!i_{D-1},i^{\prime}_{D-1}}}},\;\;\;\mbox{where}\;{{\bm{\delta}}}_{(k)}^{[j]}=[\delta_{k,i_{j}}]_{i_{j}}\end{array}

Moore-Aronszajn theorem allows us to run the argument in reverse: any
symmetric, positive definite
{\bf K}:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
uniquely defines an RKHS,
\mathcal{H}_{\bf K}
for which
{\bf K}
is the kernel.
From theorem
5
:

Theorem 6
.

The set
\mathcal{F}_{\Delta}\!=\!\{{\Delta}{\bf f}(\cdot;{\Delta}{\Theta}):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}|{\Delta}{\Theta}\in\mathbb{W}_{\Delta}\}
of changes in network behavior satisfying our
assumptions, including the bound, lies in an RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\bf K}
(that
is,
\mathcal{F}_{\Delta}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\bf K}
)
with kernel
{\bf K}={\bf I}_{m}K_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm LiNK}}
, where

K_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm LiNK}}={K}^{[D-1]}
, is the Local-intrinsic Neural Kernel (LiNK),
\forall j
:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{rl}{K}^{[j]}\!\left({\bf x},\!{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)&\!\!\!\!={\gamma}^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}\in{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}}^{[j]}}\left[\frac{\Sigma^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)}{\tilde{{\omega}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}}+\frac{1}{{\tilde{\psi}}_{{\Delta}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}}\mathbb{E}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}\left[{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\frac{{{{{\rho}}}}_{(\tilde{{\omega}})\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}}{{\gamma}^{2}+1}{K}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right);{x}_{i_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]},{x}_{i_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}}}^{\prime[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\right)\right]\right]\\
\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(16)

and
K_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm LiNK}}^{[-1]}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})=0
and

{\Sigma}^{[j]}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})
is the NNGP kernel. Moreover:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathop{\lim}\limits_{\eta\to 1}\mathbb{E}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}\left[{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\frac{{{{{\rho}}}}_{(\tilde{{\omega}})\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}}{{\gamma}^{2}+1}{K}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right);{x}_{i_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]},{x}_{i_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}}}^{\prime[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\right)\right]=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{q\geq 1}\theta_{l}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)\left(\frac{{{{{\rho}}}}_{(\tilde{{\omega}})\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}}{{\gamma}^{2}+1}{K}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)\right)^{q}\\
\theta_{q}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}\right)=\mathbb{E}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}\left[\frac{1}{q!}\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j](q)}\left({x}_{i_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\right)\frac{1}{q!}\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j](q)}\left({x}_{i_{{\widetilde{\jmath}}}}^{\prime[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\right)\right]\end{array}}}

(17)

(here
\theta_{q}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})
is the raw covariance of the

q^{\rm th}
derivative of link
({\widetilde{\jmath}},j)
’s activation given

{\bf x}
,
{\bf x}^{\prime}
.)

The proof is somewhat long and is provided in supplementary
C.3
.
Note that the NTK is essentially (with some additional scaling factors) a
first-order (in
q
) approximation of the LiNK. Assuming random initialization
the LiNK is well-defined for almost all
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{X}
if
\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
for almost all
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{X}
.
Note however that
\mathcal{F}_{\Delta}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\bf K}
- ie.
\mathcal{F}_{\Delta}
is not an RKHS in
general, but rather a subspace inside of one. Nor can we meaningfully replace

\mathcal{F}_{\Delta}
with its span or completion, as this will contain
elements that do not correspond to physically realizable networks.
Nevertheless, as we show in section
6
, we can use the local model to
construct a representor theory in terms of Banach kernels that, while related
to the LiNK, differ in certain important respects.

5.2
Application: Bounding Rademacher Complexity for Neural Network Adaptation

Like the global model, an obvious application of the local dual model is the
bounding of Rademacher complexity. The following result may be viewed as the
local analogue of our previous bound:

Theorem 7
.

The set
\mathcal{F}_{\Delta}=\{{\Delta}f(\cdot;{\Delta}{\Theta}):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}|{\Delta}{\Theta}\in\mathbb{W}_{\Delta}\}
of change in neural-network operation satisfying (
15
)
has Rademacher complexity
\mathcal{R}_{N}(\mathcal{F})\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}{{\phi}}_{{\Delta}}{{\psi}}_{{\Delta}}
(defined in Figure

2
).

The proof follows the template of
(
Bartlett & Mendelson, 2002
)
using the local feature map and
the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (see supplementary
D
).

{{\psi}}_{{\Delta}}
scales with
{\mu}_{\Delta}
, so
methods such as LoRA
(
Hu et al., 2021
)
may be expected to get
tighter bounds by restricting the weight update rank.
As an aside for future investigation we note that
spectral analysis of the LiNK could, in principle, be used to bound local
Rademacher complexity
(
Cortes et al., 2013
;
Bartlett et al., 2005
)
up to
\mathcal{O}({1}/{N})
.

6
Exact Representor Theory

In this section we construct a representor theory for the change in
neural-network operation
{\Delta}{\bf f}(\cdot;{\Delta}{\Theta})

for layer-wise neural networks. The representor theory may be viewed as an
extension of the standard NTK-based approximate representor theory to an
exact, higher-order model. Distinct from standard representor theory, our
representor theory is expressed in terms of weighted trees of height
D
with
labeled leaves. Inverting the structure of the layer-wise neural network,
the root node of these trees is node
D-1
, and “layers” (working down from
the root) are
D-2,\ldots,1,0,-1
. Precisely:

Definition 3
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=\{{\bf x}_{\{l\}}:l\in\mathbb{N}_{N}\}
,
{\bf e},{\bf f}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{M}
,
{{\bm{\varsigma}}}\in\mathbb{N}_{N}^{f_{0}f_{1}\ldots}
. Then
\mathcal{E}{}^{\lfloor\!{\bf e},{\bf f},{{\bm{\varsigma}}}\!\rceil\!}
denotes a weighted tree of height
M
with root node
M-1
; layers

M-2,\ldots,0,-1
; edge weights (per layer)
e_{M-1},e_{M-2},\ldots,e_{0}
;
fan-outs (per layer)
f_{M-1},f_{M-2},\ldots,f_{0}
; and leaf labels
{\bf x}_{\{\varsigma_{0}\}}
,
{\bf x}_{\{\varsigma_{1}\}}
,
\ldots
. Sub-trees are
denoted
\mathcal{E}{}^{\lfloor\!{\bf e},{\bf f},{{\bm{\varsigma}}}\!\rceil\!}_{k}=\mathcal{E}{}^{\lfloor\!{\bf e}_{{\backslash}M-1},{\bf f}_{{\backslash}M-1},{{\bm{\varsigma}}}_{\backslash f_{M-2}\ldots{f_{1}f_{0}k}:f_{M-2}\ldots{f_{1}f_{0}(k+1)-1}}\!\rceil\!}
where
k\in\mathbb{N}{}_{f_{M-1}}
.

We use the shorthand
{\{{\bf x}\}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\bf e},{\bf 1},-}

where the data label is superfluous. Our key result for this section is:

Theorem 8
.

Let
{\Delta}{\bf f}:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{W}_{\Delta}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the change in neural network operation (
13
)
satisfying the constraints given in theorem
5
. For
a dense layer-wide feedforward network, assume
{\Delta}{\Theta}
is
generated by gradient a descent step on unregularized risk
on training set
\mathcal{D}
. Then:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{l}{\Delta}{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Delta}{\Theta}\right)\!=\!\!\mathop{\sum}\limits_{{\bf k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{D}}\!\!\!\frac{\left(-\eta\right)^{k_{0}k_{1}\ldots}}{{\bf k}!}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathop{\sum}\limits_{{\bf p}\in\mathbb{N}_{N}^{k_{0}k_{1}\ldots}}\!\!\!\!\!\!{\bf K}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm LeNK}}\!\left(\!\left\{\!\,{\bf x}\,\!\right\}^{\lfloor\!{\bf k},{\bf 1},-\!\rceil\!}\!\!,\mathcal{D}^{\lfloor\!{\bf 1},{\bf k},{\bf p}\!\rceil\!};{\Theta}+{\Delta}{\Theta},{\Theta}\right)\!{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{p_{0}\!\}}\!\otimes\!{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{p_{1}\!\}}\!\otimes\!\ldots\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(18)

where
{\bf k}!=k_{0}!k_{1}!\ldots
and
{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{l\}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

\forall l\in\mathbb{N}_{N}
. Here
{\bf K}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm LeNK}}
is the (matrix-valued) Local-extrinsic
Neural Kernel (LeNK) on the space of weighted trees (definition

3
), defined as
{\bf K}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm LeNK}}={\bf K}^{[D-1]}
:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{l}\resizebox{21479355}{}{${\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{r}{\bf K}^{[j]}\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime\lfloor{\bf e}^{\prime},{\bf f}^{\prime},{{\bm{\varsigma}}}^{\prime}\rceil},{\mathcal{E}}^{\lfloor{\bf e},{\bf f},{{\bm{\varsigma}}}\rceil};{\Theta}^{\prime},{\Theta}\right)=\delta_{{\bf e}^{\prime}}^{{\bf 1}}\delta_{{\bf f}^{\prime}}^{{\bf 1}}\delta_{{\bf e}}^{{\bf 1}}\delta_{{\bf f}}^{{\bf 1}}{\bf I}\Sigma^{[j]}\left({\bf x}^{\prime}_{\{\varsigma^{\prime}_{0}\}},{\bf x}_{\{\varsigma_{0}\}}\right)+\Big(\mathop{\bigotimes}\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{f^{\prime}_{j}}}\Big(\mathop{\rm diag}\limits_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}\left(\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j](e^{\prime}_{j})}\left({\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{\prime\lfloor{\bf e}^{\prime},{\bf f}^{\prime},{{\bm{\varsigma}}}^{\prime}\rceil}{}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}\right)\right){\bf W}^{\prime[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\Big)\Big)^{\rm T}\\
\ldots\Big(\mathop{\bigotimes^{\updownarrow}}\limits_{k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{f^{\prime}_{j}}}\mathop{\bigotimes^{\leftrightarrow}}\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{f_{j}}}{\bf K}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime\lfloor{\bf e}^{\prime},{\bf f}^{\prime},{{\bm{\varsigma}}}^{\prime}\rceil}_{k^{\prime}},{\mathcal{E}}^{\lfloor{\bf e},{\bf f},{{\bm{\varsigma}}}\rceil}_{k};{\Theta}^{\prime},{\Theta}\right)\Big)\Big(\mathop{\bigotimes}\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{f_{j}}}\Big(\mathop{\rm diag}\limits_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}\left(\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j](e_{j})}\left({\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{\lfloor{\bf e},{\bf f},{{\bm{\varsigma}}}\rceil}{}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}\right)\right){\bf W}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\Big)\Big)\end{array}}}$}\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(19)

recursively, with
{\bf K}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm LeNK}}^{[-1]}=0
;
5
5

5

Recall that
{\bf A}\otimes^{\updownarrow}{\bf B}
and
{\bf A}\otimes^{\leftrightarrow}{\bf B}
denote, respectively, the column- and
row-wise Kronecker products.
and
\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j](\varepsilon)}({\mathcal{E}}^{\lfloor{\bf e},{\bf f},{{\bm{\varsigma}}}\rceil}{}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}})=\prod_{r}\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j](\varepsilon)}(x_{\{\varsigma_{r}\}i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}})
.
Furthermore:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{l}\left.\begin{array}[]{l}{\Delta}{\bf W}^{[j-1,j]}\!=\!\sum_{l}{\tilde{\bf x}}_{\{l\}}^{[j-1,j]}{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{l\}}^{[j]{\rm T}}\\
{\Delta}{\bf b}^{[j]}\!=\!\sum_{l}{\gamma}{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{l\}}^{[j]}\\
\end{array}\!\!\!\right|\!\!\begin{array}[]{l}{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{l\}}^{[j-1]}\!=\!\mathop{\rm diag}\limits_{{i\in\mathbb{N}_{m}}}\left(\tau^{[j-1,j](1)}\!\left({x}_{\{l\}i}^{[j-1]}\right)\right)\!\!{\bf W}^{[j-1,j]}{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{l\}}^{[j]}\forall j>0\\
{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{l\}}^{[D-1]}\!=\!{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{l\}}=\nabla_{{\bf x}_{\{l\}}^{[D-1]}}L\left({\bf x}_{\{l\}}^{[D-1]}-{\bf y}_{\{l\}}\right)\\
\end{array}\\
\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(20)

A proof of this theorem is given in supplementary
E
. We now
consider the novel characteristics of this representor theory (and the LeNK in
particular) and give successive approximations connecting with the LiNK and
NTK regime:

Kernel warping:
As may be observed from (
18
) and
(
19
), the weight-step itself is a part of the
kernel definition. This arises due to interactions between
{\Delta}{\tilde{\bf x}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}
and
{\Delta}{\bf W}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}
. For a sufficiently small weight-step it is reasonable to
approximate
{\Theta}+{\Delta}{\Theta}\approx{\Theta}
, in which case (
18
) simplifies to:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{l}{\Delta}{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Delta}{\Theta}\right)\!\approx\!{\sum}_{\bf k}\frac{\left(-\eta\right)^{k_{0}k_{1}\ldots}}{{\bf k}!}{\sum}_{\bf p}{\bf K}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm LeNK}}\!\left(\left\{{\bf x}\right\}^{\lfloor{\bf k},{\bf 1},-\rceil},\mathcal{D}^{\lfloor{\bf 1},{\bf k},{\bf p}\rceil};{\Theta},{\Theta}\right){{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{p_{0}\}}\!\otimes\!{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{p_{1}\}}\!\otimes\!\ldots\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(21)

which is more conventional insofar as the kernel is “fixed” (not a
function of
{\Delta}{\Theta}
).

Cross terms:
Unlike the LiNK and the NTK, the LeNK is an unavoidably
matrix-valued kernel. In the wide-network limit, after Gaussian
initialization, the off-diagonal terms will tend to
0
, so:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{l}{\Delta}{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Delta}{\Theta}\right)\approx{\sum}_{\bf k}\frac{\left(-\eta\right)^{k_{0}k_{1}\ldots}}{{\bf k}!}{\sum}_{\bf p}{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm LeNK}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm NCT}}\left(\left\{{\bf x}\right\}^{\lfloor{\bf k},{\bf 1},-\rceil},\mathcal{D}^{\lfloor{\bf 1},{\bf k},{\bf p}\rceil}\right){{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{p_{0}\}}\odot{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{p_{1}\}}\odot\ldots\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

(22)

where
{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm LeNK}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm NCT}}=K^{[D-1]}
is defined by recursively by (with
K^{[-1]}=0
,
{\widetilde{\jmath}}=j-1
):

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{l}{K}^{[j]}\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime\lfloor\ldots\rceil},{\mathcal{E}}^{\lfloor\ldots\rceil}\right)=\delta_{{\bf e}^{\prime}}^{{\bf 1}}\delta_{{\bf f}^{\prime}}^{{\bf 1}}\delta_{{\bf e}}^{{\bf 1}}\delta_{{\bf f}}^{{\bf 1}}\Sigma^{[j]}\left({\bf x}^{\prime}_{\!\{\varsigma^{\prime}_{0}\}}\!,{\bf x}_{\!\{\varsigma_{0}\}}\right)\!+\!\mathop{\prod}\limits_{k^{\prime},k}\theta^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}_{\!(e^{\prime}_{j}\!,e_{j}\!)}\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime\lfloor\ldots\rceil}_{k^{\prime}}\!,{\mathcal{E}}^{\lfloor\ldots\rceil}_{k}\right){K}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}}]}\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime\lfloor\ldots\rceil}_{k^{\prime}}\!,{\mathcal{E}}^{\lfloor\ldots\rceil}_{k}\right)\\
\theta^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}_{(e^{\prime},e)}\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime\lfloor\ldots\rceil}_{k^{\prime}},{\mathcal{E}}^{\lfloor\ldots\rceil}_{k}\right)=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}{W}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}},i_{j}}^{\prime[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j](e^{\prime})}\left({\mathcal{E}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime\lfloor\ldots\rceil}{}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}\right)\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j](e)}\left({\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{\lfloor\ldots\rceil}{}_{i_{\widetilde{\jmath}}}\right)\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

Higher-order Interactions:
Note that (
22
)
includes terms that mix the contributions of multiple training vectors

{\bf x}_{\{l\}}
. If we remove these terms from (
22
)
we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\Delta}{\bf f}\left({\bf x};{\Delta}{\Theta}\right)\approx{\sum}_{\bf k}\frac{\left(-\eta\right)^{k_{0}k_{1}\ldots}}{{\bf k}!}{\sum}_{p}{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm LeNK}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm NCT}}\left(\left\{{\bf x}\right\}^{\lfloor{\bf k},{\bf 1},-\rceil},\mathcal{D}^{\lfloor{\bf 1},{\bf k},p{\bf 1}_{k_{0}k_{1}\ldots}\rceil}\right){{\bm{\alpha}}}_{\{p\}}^{\odot k_{0}k_{1}\ldots}\end{array}

(23)

This approximation is reasonable in the wide-network limit where the
size of the weight-step - and hence the size of
{{\bm{\alpha}}}
- is
small, so the linear term
{\bf k}={\bf 1}
dominates.
The
{\bf k}={\bf 1}
term of
{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm LeNK}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm NCT}}
is equivalent to the NTK
up to scale factors; and moreover if we apply the approach of

(
Saitoh, 1998
)
to (
23
) we may obtain theorem

6
and the LiNK (again up to scale factors).

We note that higher-order statistical terms have been studied previously (eg.

(
Bai & Lee, 2019
)
), while off-diagonal terms may be viewed as an artifact of
working in the non-over-parameterized regime; however to the best of our
knowledge ours is the first paper to introduce the idea of kernel warping and
describe its role in an exact representor theory.

7
Conclusions and Future Directions

In this paper we have presented two models of neural networks and neural
network training for neural network of arbitrary width, depth and topology.
First we presented an exact (non-approximated) RKBS model of the overall
network in the form of a bilinear product between a data- and weight- feature
map. We have used this model to construct a bound on Rademacher complexity,
which is particularly tight for feedforward ReLU networks. The second model
we have presented models the
change
in the neural network due to a
bounded change in weights and biases. This model cast the change in RKHS
with the local-intrinsic neural kernel (LiNK). We have shown that this can be
used to bound Rademacher complexity for network adaptation. Finally, for
unregularized gradient descent, we have provided an exact representor theory
in terms of a kernel (the local-extrinsic neural kernel (LeNK)), providing
insight into the role of model shift (kernel warping), cross (off-diagonal)
terms and higher-order interaction between training vectors. We have
demonstrated that the LiNK is, in a sense, an approximation of the LeNK when
cross-products and kernel warping are neglected; and moreover that the NTK is
a first-order approximation of the LiNK. We have also discussed the role of
weight initialization and implications for feedforward ReLU networks and
residual networks (ResNets).
We foresee a number of possible extensions to this work. Most obviously it
would be informative to derive the LeNK and LiNK in closed form for common
neural activations, which may subsequently be subject to spectral analysis.
The exact representor theory needs to be generalized to arbitrary topologies,
and the impact of regularization incorporated. Finally, we intend to use our
analysis to study the convergence of neural networks in terms of (corrected
for higher-order terms) gradient flow.

Appendix A
Properties of Hermite polynomials

The (probabilist’s) Hermite polynomials are given by
(
Abramowitz et al., 1972
;
Morse & Feshbach, 1953
;
Olver et al., 2010
;
Courant & Hilbert, 1937
)
:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\begin{array}[]{rl}{H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)&\!\!\!\!=\left(-1\right)^{k}e^{\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}}\frac{d^{k}}{d\zeta^{k}}e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}}\\
\end{array}&\forall k\in\mathbb{N}\end{array}

or, explicitly:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\begin{array}[]{rl}{H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)&\!\!\!\!=k!\mathop{\sum}\limits_{0\leq 2p\leq k}\frac{\left(-1\right)^{p}}{2^{p}p!(k-2p)!}\zeta^{k-2p}\\
\end{array}&\forall k\in\mathbb{N}\end{array}

(24)

and form an orthogonal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},e^{-x^{2}})
. Thus for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},e^{-x^{2}})
there exist Hermite coefficients
a_{0},a_{1},\ldots\in\mathbb{R}
(i.e. the Hermite transform of
f
) so that:

\begin{array}[]{rl}f\left(\zeta\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}a_{k}{H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)\;\;\;\forall\zeta\in\mathbb{R}\end{array}

where:

\begin{array}[]{rl}a_{k}&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{k!\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f\left(\zeta\right)e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}}{H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta\\
\end{array}

This series representation converges everywhere on the real line. Moreover

(
Hille, 1940
;
Boyd, 1980
)
this series converges on a strip
\mathbb{S}_{\rho}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:-\rho<{\rm Im}(z)<\rho\}
of width
\rho
about the
real axis in the complex plane, where:
6
6

6

Note that
(
Hille, 1940
;
Boyd, 1980
)

use the normalized physicist’s Hermite polynomials. The additional scale
factor here arises in the translation to the un-normalized probabilist’s
Hermite polynomials used here.

\begin{array}[]{rl}\rho=-\mathop{\rm lim\;sup}\limits_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2k+1}}\log\left(\left|\frac{a_{k}}{\sqrt{k!\sqrt{\pi}}}\right|\right)\end{array}

(25)

The Hermite numbers derive from the Hermite polynomials:
7
7

7

Typically
the Hermite numbers are defined from the physicist’s Hermite polynomials, but as
we use the Probabilist’s form we find these more convenient.

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\rm He}_{k}&\!\!\!\!\triangleq{H\!e}_{k}\left(0\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{if $k$ odd}\\
\frac{k!}{(\frac{k}{2})!}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\frac{k}{2}}&\mbox{if $k$ even}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{array}

It is well known that (see e.g.
(
Morse & Feshbach, 1953
)
):

\begin{array}[]{rl}{H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta+\xi\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{0\leq l\leq k}\binom{k}{l}{H\!e}_{k-l}\left(\zeta\right)\xi^{l}\\
\end{array}

and so:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{0\leq l\leq k}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}\zeta^{l}\\
\end{array}

It follows that, taking care not to change or order of summation (remember
this is an alternating series):

\begin{array}[]{rl}f\left(\zeta\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=0}^{k}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}\zeta^{l}\\
\end{array}

Next we derive a helpful property involving
rectified
Hermite
expansions. Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},e^{-x^{2}})
,
f(0)=0
, then:

\begin{array}[]{rll}f\left(x\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}{H\!e}\left(x\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l}\\
\end{array}

Denoting the imaginary element
{\tt i}
:

\begin{array}[]{rl}f\left({\tt i}x\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}\left({\tt i}x\right)^{l}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=0}^{k-1}\binom{k}{k-l}{\rm He}_{l}\left({\tt i}x\right)^{k-l}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}{\tt i}^{k}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=0}^{k-1}\binom{k}{k-l}\left({\tt i}^{l}{\rm He}_{l}\right)x^{k-l}\\
\end{array}

Recall that
{\rm He}_{l}=0
for
l=1,3,5,\ldots
, and
\sgn({\rm He}_{2p})=(-1)^{p}
. Therefore

\begin{array}[]{rl}f\left({\tt i}x\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}{\tt i}^{k}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=0}^{k-1}\binom{k}{k-l}\left|{\rm He}_{l}\right|x^{k-l}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}{\tt i}^{k}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}\left|{\rm He}_{k-l}\right|x^{l}\\
\end{array}

and so:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\rm Im}\left(f\left({\tt i}x\right)\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1,3,5,\ldots}\left(-1\right)^{\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}\left|{\rm He}_{k-l}\right|x^{l}\\
{\rm Re}\left(f\left({\tt i}x\right)\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=2,4,6,\ldots}\left(-1\right)^{\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}\left|{\rm He}_{k-l}\right|x^{l}\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\rm Im}\left(f\left({\tt i}x\right)\right)+{\rm Re}\left(f\left({\tt i}x\right)\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(-1\right)^{\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}\left|{\rm He}_{k-l}\right|x^{l}\\
\end{array}

(26)

Finally we make some observations regarding derivatives that will be required later.
Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},e^{-x^{2}})
,
f(0)=0
. Then:

\begin{array}[]{rll}f\left(x\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l}\\
\end{array}

and subsequently:

\begin{array}[]{rll}f^{(1)}\left(x\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}l\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l-1}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}l\frac{k!}{l!(k-l)!}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l-1}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}k\frac{(k-1)!}{(l-1)!(k-l)!}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l-1}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}k\binom{k-1}{l-1}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l-1}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)a_{k+1}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=0}^{k-1}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l}\\
\end{array}

and:

\begin{array}[]{rll}f^{(2)}\left(x\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)a_{k+1}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k-1}(l-1)\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l-1}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)a_{k+1}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k-1}(l-1)\frac{k!}{l!(k-l)!}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l-1}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)a_{k+1}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k-1}k\frac{(k-1)!}{(l-l)!(k-l)!}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l-1}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}(k+1)a_{k+1}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k-1}k\frac{(k-1)!}{(l-l)!(k-l)!}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l-1}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)(k+2)a_{k+2}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=0}^{k-2}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l}\\
\end{array}

and so on to:

\begin{array}[]{rll}f^{(n)}\left(x\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(k+n)!}{k!}a_{k+n}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=0}^{k-n}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}x^{l}\\
\end{array}

A.1
Activation Functions

Following the previous method we introduce our notation for the activation
functions. Recall
\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},e^{-x^{2}})
by
assumption. Subsequently
{\overline{\tau}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},e^{-x^{2}})
, where:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\overline{\tau}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi\right)=\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\xi+\zeta\right)-\tau^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\xi\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}a^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}_{(\xi)k}{H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)\;\;\;\forall\zeta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}_{(\xi)k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=0}^{k}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}\zeta^{l}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}a^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}_{(\xi)k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}\zeta^{l}\\
\end{array}

(27)

(in the final step we use that
{\overline{\tau}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}(0;\xi)=0
) with coefficients:

\begin{array}[]{rl}a^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}_{(\xi)k}&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{k!\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\overline{\tau}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi\right)e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}}{H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta\\
\end{array}

which converges on a strip
\mathbb{S}_{\rho_{(\xi)}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:-\rho_{(\xi)}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}<{\rm Im}(z)<\rho_{(\xi)}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\}
of width
\rho_{(\xi)}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}
about the
real axis in the complex plane, where:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\rho_{(\xi)}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}=-\mathop{\rm lim\;sup}\limits_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2k+1}}\log\left(\left|\frac{a^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}_{(\xi)k}}{\sqrt{k!\sqrt{\pi}}}\right|\right)\end{array}

A.2
Rectified Activation Functions

Recall that the rectified activation functions are defined as:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{rl}{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi,\xi^{\prime}\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{a_{(\xi)k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}a_{(\xi^{\prime})k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}}{\eta^{2k}}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}{\binom{k}{l}}^{2}{\rm He}_{k-l}^{2}\zeta^{l}\\
\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}

where
\eta\in(0,1)
is fixed.
To understand the convergence of this function, observe that:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi,\xi^{\prime}\right)&\!\!\!\!\leq\mathop{\max}\limits_{\xi^{\prime\prime}\in\{\xi,\xi^{\prime}\}}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\left|\frac{a_{(\xi^{\prime\prime})k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}}{\eta^{k}}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}\sqrt{\zeta^{l}}\right|^{2}\end{array}

which is the
2
-norm of a sequence. Hence:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi,\xi^{\prime}\right)&\!\!\!\!\leq\mathop{\max}\limits_{\xi^{\prime\prime}\in\{\xi,\xi^{\prime}\}}\left(\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\left|\frac{\left|a_{(\xi^{\prime\prime})k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\right|}{\eta^{k}}\binom{k}{l}\left|{\rm He}_{k-l}\right|\sqrt{\zeta^{l}}\right|\right)^{2}\end{array}

Thus is suffices to study the convergence of:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\gamma^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\lambda;\xi\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left|a_{(\xi)k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\right|}{\eta^{k}}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}\left|{\rm He}_{k-l}\right|\lambda^{l}\end{array}

which in turn bounds:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi,\xi^{\prime}\right)&\!\!\!\!\leq\max\left\{\gamma^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\sqrt{\zeta};\xi\right)^{2},\gamma^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\sqrt{\zeta};\xi^{\prime}\right)^{2}\right\}\end{array}

Using (
26
):

{\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{rl}\gamma^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\lambda;\xi\right)&\!\!\!\!={\rm Re}\left(\overline{\gamma}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left({\tt i}\lambda;\xi\right)\right)+{\rm Im}\left(\overline{\gamma}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left({\tt i}\lambda;\xi\right)\right)\end{array}}\!\!\!\!}

where:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\gamma^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\lambda;\xi\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left|a_{(\xi)k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\right|}{\eta^{k}}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}{\rm He}_{k-l}\lambda^{l}\end{array}

which, using (
25
), is convergent for:

\begin{array}[]{l}\left|\lambda\right|<L_{{(\xi)}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}<-\mathop{\rm lim\;sup}\limits_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2k+1}}\left(\ln\left|\frac{a_{(\xi)k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}}{\eta^{k}}\right|\right)\\
\end{array}

whereon:

\begin{array}[]{l}\left|\gamma^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\lambda;\xi\right)\right|\leq M_{{(\xi)}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}=\gamma^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(L_{{(\xi)}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]};\xi\right)\end{array}

and we conclude that
{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}(\zeta;\xi,\xi^{\prime})
is convergent
for:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\left|\zeta\right|\leq{{{{\rho}}}}_{{(\xi)\eta}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}=\min\left\{-\mathop{\rm lim\;sup}\limits_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2k+1}}\ln\left|\frac{a_{(\xi)k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}}{\eta^{k}}\right|,-\mathop{\rm lim\;sup}\limits_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2k+1}}\ln\left|\frac{a_{(\xi)k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}}{\eta^{k}}\right|\right\}^{2}\end{array}

whereon:

\begin{array}[]{l}{{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left(\zeta;\xi,\xi^{\prime}\right)\leq{{{T}}}_{{(\xi)\eta}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}={{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left({{{{\rho}}}}_{{(\xi)\eta}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2};\xi,\xi^{\prime}\right)\end{array}

The envelope is convergent for:

\begin{array}[]{l}\left|\zeta\right|<{{{{\rho}}}}_{({\omega})\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}=\mathop{\rm inf}\limits_{|\xi|,|\xi^{\prime}|\leq{\omega}}{{{{\rho}}}}_{{(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\eta}}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}\\
\end{array}

whereon:

\begin{array}[]{l}{{{T}}}_{({\omega})\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2}={{{\hat{\tau}}}}_{\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}\left({{{{\rho}}}}_{({\omega})\eta}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]2};{\omega}\right)\end{array}

Finally:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{{{\hat{s}}}}_{\eta}\left(\zeta\right)&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\eta^{2k}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{l=1}^{k}\zeta^{l}=\frac{\zeta}{1-\zeta}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\eta^{2k}\left(1-\zeta^{k}\right)=\frac{\zeta}{1-\zeta}\left(\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\eta^{2k}-\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}(\zeta\eta^{2})^{k}\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!=\frac{\zeta}{1-\zeta}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}}{1-\eta^{2}}-\frac{\zeta\eta^{2}}{1-\zeta\eta^{2}}\right)\end{array}

is convergent
\forall|\zeta|<{{{{R}}}}_{\eta}^{2}<1
, with max
value of
{{{{S}}}}_{\eta}^{2}=\frac{{{{{R}}}}_{\eta}^{2}}{1-{{{{R}}}}_{\eta}^{2}}(\frac{\eta^{2}}{1-\eta^{2}}-\frac{\eta^{2}{{{{R}}}}_{\eta}^{2}}{1-\eta^{2}{{{{R}}}}_{\eta}^{2}})

thereon.

A.3
ReLU Activation Function

In this section we derive the Hermite-polynomial expansion of the centered
ReLU activation function:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\overline{\tau}}^{[{\rm ReLU}]}\left(\zeta;\xi\right)&\!\!\!\!=\tau^{[{\rm ReLU}]}\left(\xi+\zeta\right)-\tau^{[{\rm ReLU}]}\left(\xi\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\zeta+\xi&\mbox{if }\zeta>-\xi\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\\
\end{array}\right.-\left[\xi\right]_{+}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{(\xi)k}^{[{\widetilde{\jmath}},j]}{H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)\\
\end{array}

We find it convenient to work in terms of the physicists Hermite
polynomials
H_{k}
to suit
(
Gradshteyn & Ryzhik, 2000
)
. Using this:

{{\begin{array}[]{l}\resizebox{22609920}{}{${{\begin{array}[]{rl}a_{(\xi)k}^{[{\rm ReLU}]}&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}k!}\int_{-\xi}^{\infty}\left(\zeta+\xi\right)e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}}{H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}k!}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\xi\right]_{+}e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}}{H\!e}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta\\
&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}k!}\int_{-\xi}^{\infty}\left(\zeta+\xi\right)e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}}H_{k}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{2}}\right)d\zeta-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}k!}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\xi\right]_{+}e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}}H_{k}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{2}}\right)d\zeta\\
&\!\!\!\!=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{-\sqrt{2}\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{2}}+\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)e^{-\left(\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}}H_{k}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{2}}\right)d\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{2}}-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right]_{+}e^{-\left(\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}}H_{k}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{2}}\right)d\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{2}}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}k!}\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\left(\zeta+\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}k!}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right]_{+}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta\\
&\!\!\!\!=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}k!}\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}\zeta H_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}k!}\xi\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}k!}\left[\xi\right]_{+}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta\\
\end{array}}}$}\end{array}}}

Using the recursion and derivative properties, for
k>1
:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\zeta H_{k}\left(\zeta\right)&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{2}H_{k+1}\left(\zeta\right)+\frac{1}{2}H^{\prime}_{k}\left(\zeta\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{2}H_{k+1}\left(\zeta\right)+kH_{k-1}\left(\zeta\right)\\
\end{array}

and hence, using
(
Gradshteyn & Ryzhik, 2000
, (7.373))
:

\begin{array}[]{rl}a_{(\xi)k}^{[{\rm ReLU}]}&\!\!\!\!=\frac{k+1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k+1}(k+1)!}\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k+1}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k-1}(k-1)!}\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k-1}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta+\ldots\\
&\;\;\;\;\ldots+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}k!}\xi\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}k!}\left[\xi\right]_{+}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta\\
&\!\!\!\!=\frac{k+1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k+1}(k+1)!}\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k+1}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k-1}(k-1)!}\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k-1}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta+\ldots\\
&\;\;\;\;\ldots+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}k!}\xi\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta-\delta_{k,2p}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p}(2p)!}\left[\xi\right]_{+}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{2p}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta\\
&\!\!\!\!=\frac{k+1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k+1}(k+1)!}\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k+1}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k-1}(k-1)!}\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k-1}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta+\ldots\\
&\;\;\;\;\ldots+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}k!}\xi\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}H_{k}\left(\zeta\right)d\zeta\end{array}

Using
(
Gradshteyn & Ryzhik, 2000
, (7.373))
we have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}a_{(\xi)k}^{[{\rm ReLU}]}&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k+1}(k+1)!}(k+1)\left(e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}H_{k}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)-e^{-\frac{\infty^{2}}{2}}H_{k}\left(\frac{\infty}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right)+\ldots\\
&\;\;\;\;\ldots\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k-1}(k-1)!}\left(e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}H_{k-2}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)-e^{-\frac{\infty^{2}}{2}}H_{k-2}\left(\frac{\infty}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right)+\ldots\\
&\;\;\;\;\ldots\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}k!}\xi\left(e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}H_{k-1}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)-e^{-\frac{\infty^{2}}{2}}H_{k-1}\left(\frac{\infty}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}\left(\frac{k+1}{\sqrt{2}^{k}(k+1)!}H_{k}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k-2}(k-1)!}H_{k-2}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{k-1}k!}\xi H_{k-1}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right)\end{array}

If
k=2p
and
p>0
then, noting that
H_{k}(0)=\sqrt{2}^{k}{\rm He}_{k}
:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{rl}a_{(\xi)2p}^{[{\rm ReLU}]}&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p}(2p+1)!}(2p+1)H_{2p}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p-2}(2p-1)!}H_{2p-2}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p-1}(2p)!}\xi H_{2p-1}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p}(2p+1)!}(2p+1)H_{2p}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p-2}(2p-1)!}H_{2p-2}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p-1}(2p)!}\xi H_{2p-1}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!=\frac{(-1)^{p+1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}(2p-1)2^{p}p!}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}\left(\frac{(-1)^{p+1}(2p-1)p!}{(2p)!}H_{2p}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\frac{(-1)^{p+1}2p!}{(2(p-1))!}H_{2p-2}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)-\frac{(-1)^{p+1}(p-1)!}{\sqrt{2}(2(p-1))!}\xi H_{2p-1}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!\left(=\frac{(-1)^{p+1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}(2p-1)2^{p}p!}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}\left(\frac{(-1)^{p+1}(2p-1)p!}{(2p)!}H_{2p}\left(0\right)+\frac{(-1)^{p+1}2p!}{(2(p-1))!}H_{2p-2}\left(0\right)\right),\mbox{ if }\xi=0\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!\left(=\frac{(-1)^{p+1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}(2p-1)2^{p}p!}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}\left(\frac{(-1)^{p+1}(2p-1)p!}{(2p)!}2^{p}{\rm He}_{2p}+\frac{(-1)^{p+1}2p!}{(2(p-1))!}2^{p-1}{\rm He}_{2p-2}\right),\mbox{ if }\xi=0\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!\left(=\frac{(-1)^{p+1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}(2p-1)2^{p}p!}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}\left(\frac{(-1)^{p+1}(2p-1)p!}{(2p)!}2^{p}\frac{(-1)^{p}(2p)!}{2^{p}p!}+\frac{(-1)^{p+1}2p!}{(2(p-1))!}2^{p-1}\frac{(-1)^{p+1}(2p-2)!}{(p-1)!2^{p-1}}\right),\mbox{ if }\xi=0\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!\left(=\frac{(-1)^{p+1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}(2p-1)2^{p}p!},\mbox{ if }\xi=0\right)\\
\end{array}}}

If
k=2p+1
and
p>0
then:

{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\begin{array}[]{rl}a_{(\xi)2p+1}^{[{\rm ReLU}]}&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p+1}(2p+2)!}(2p+2)H_{2p+1}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p-1}(2p)!}H_{2p-1}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p-2}(2p-1)!}\xi H_{2p-2}\left(-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p+1}(2p+2)!}(2p+2)H_{2p+1}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p-1}(2p)!}H_{2p-1}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{2p-2}(2p-1)!}\xi H_{2p-2}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}\left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2^{p+1}(2p+1)!}H_{2p+1}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2^{p}(2p)!}H_{2p-1}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{2^{p-1}(2p-1)!}\xi H_{2p-2}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!\left(=0\mbox{ if }\xi=0\right)\\
\end{array}}}

For the cases
k=0,1
we use the result:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\int_{a}^{b}x^{m}e^{-x^{2}}dx=\frac{1}{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{m+1}{2},a^{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{m+1}{2},b^{2}\right)\end{array}

and so:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\int_{a}^{\infty}x^{m}e^{-x^{2}}dx=\frac{1}{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{m+1}{2},a^{2}\right)\end{array}

In the case
k=0
:

\begin{array}[]{rl}a_{(\xi)0}^{[{\rm ReLU}]}&\!\!\!\!=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\zeta e^{-\zeta^{2}}d\zeta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\xi\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\zeta^{2}}d\zeta-\left[\xi\right]_{+}\\
&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\Gamma\left(1,\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\xi\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}\right)-\left[\xi\right]_{+}\\
&\!\!\!\!\left(=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mbox{ if }\xi=0\right)\\
\end{array}

and in the case
k=1
:

\begin{array}[]{rl}a_{(\xi)1}^{[{\rm ReLU}]}&\!\!\!\!=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\zeta^{2}e^{-\zeta^{2}}d\zeta+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\xi\int_{-\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\zeta e^{-\zeta^{2}}d\zeta\\
&\!\!\!\!=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\xi\Gamma\left(1,\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}\right)\\
&\!\!\!\!\left(=\frac{1}{2}\mbox{ if }\xi=0\right)\\
\end{array}

Next we derive the magnitude functions for the ReLU. Using integration by
parts, we see that:
